========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came in because my mind just feels... foggy. Like a thick soup. I work as a library assistant, and lately, I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. Which is really strange for me, because I've always been so particular about my notes. I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? The owner, Mr. Higgins, he always had a jar of peppermints on the counter, the really strong ones. 

But, um... anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I just sit there at my desk now, and I cannot even decide what simple thing to do next. Sort the returns, check the cart... I just kind of freeze up and don't know where to start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. 

But, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.

coworkers, making lists for a lecture series, planning trips, and setting up painting projects all at once, much more than I would normally take on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional the library.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my mind just feels... foggy. Like a thick soup. I work as a library assistant, and lately, I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. Which is really strange for me, because I've always been so particular about my notes. I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... 

But, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.

She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't been doing the puzzles or much of anything else to even know if I'd enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... 

But, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.

Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.

The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel guilty about things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... 

But, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.

Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started calling former coworkers, making lists for a lecture series, planning trips, and setting up painting projects all at once, much more than I would normally take on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I have let the lecture plans, travel notes, and even simple household paperwork pile up because I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: This whole shift has been going on for about three weeks since the first rush after retirement faded.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The low mood and fogginess have been present most days for about three months, around the time I started preparing to leave the library.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. I haven't had any thoughts like that. Though, speaking of not being alive, we actually just got a massive donation at the library from the estate of Mr. Henderson. He passed away a few weeks ago, bless his heart. He lived in that gorgeous Victorian house on Elm Street, the one with the big weeping willow out in the front yard. I always admired that tree on my walk to work. We had a willow when I was a boy, right by the creek behind our property. My brother Tom and I would spend hours down there catching crawdads. We'd put them in this old, rusty Folgers coffee can. You know, the big metal ones they used to make before everything went to plastic? My mother used to use those old cans to hold her sewing notions. She had one that was just filled to the brim with spare buttons. Every color and shape you could imagine. I used to love running my hands through them when I was little, they made this wonderful clinking sound against the tin... 

But, um... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Oh, right, about... wanting to die. No, no, I don't want to die. I just feel incredibly tired, like I said, and everything feels like a chore. But I definitely haven't thought about anything like that.

I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? I just kind of freeze up and don't know where to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worthless? A burden? Well, I suppose I don't do as much around the house as I used to. My wife, Sarah, she's been taking on a lot more of the chores lately. She's a saint, really. We've been married for thirty-five years this coming October. We had our honeymoon in the Poconos, back when those heart-shaped tubs were all the rage. It was autumn, and the leaves were just spectacular. Reds and golds, you know? We took this scenic train ride through the mountains. I remember the conductor had this magnificent handlebar mustache, the kind you only see in old tintype photographs. He told us he used a special wax imported from France to keep it curled just so. I always thought about growing a mustache myself, but Sarah said it would tickle too much. She prefers me clean-shaven. Always has, ever since we met at the roller rink back in seventy-nine. They were playing Donna Summer, and she was wearing these bright yellow leg warmers...

But, um... where was I going with that? Oh, right. Feeling worthless. No, I wouldn't use those words. I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.

I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't been doing the puzzles or much of anything else to even know if I'd enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nPatient: Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. I haven't had any thoughts like that. Though, speaking of not being alive, we actually just got a massive donation at the library from the estate of Mr. Henderson. He passed away a few weeks ago, bless his heart. He lived in that gorgeous Victorian house on Elm Street, the one with the big weeping willow out in the front yard. I always admired that tree on my walk to work. We had a willow when I was a boy, right by the creek behind our property. My brother Tom and I would spend hours down there catching crawdads. We'd put them in this old, rusty Folgers coffee can. You know, the big metal ones they used to make before everything went to plastic? My mother used to use those old cans to hold her sewing notions. She had one that was just filled to the brim with spare buttons. Every color and shape you could imagine. I used to love running my hands through them when I was little, they made this wonderful clinking sound against the tin... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Oh, right, about... wanting to die. No, no, I don't want to die. I just feel incredibly tired, like I said, and everything feels like a chore. But I definitely haven't thought about anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others lately?\n\nPatient: Worthless? A burden? Well, I suppose I don't do as much around the house as I used to. My wife, Sarah, she's been taking on a lot more of the chores lately. She's a saint, really. We've been married for thirty-five years this coming October. We had our honeymoon in the Poconos, back when those heart-shaped tubs were all the rage. It was autumn, and the leaves were just spectacular. Reds and golds, you know? We took this scenic train ride through the mountains. I remember the conductor had this magnificent handlebar mustache, the kind you only see in old tintype photographs. He told us he used a special wax imported from France to keep it curled just so. I always thought about growing a mustache myself, but Sarah said it would tickle too much. She prefers me clean-shaven. Always has, ever since we met at the roller rink back in seventy-nine. They were playing Donna Summer, and she was wearing these bright yellow leg warmers...\n\nBut, um... where was I going with that? Oh, right. Feeling worthless. No, I wouldn't use those words. I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? Joy? Oh, I don't know about joy. I used to love painting, you know. Watercolors. I have this whole set of Windsor and Newton paints my daughter got me for Christmas a few years back. They come in these little half-pans, and the colors are just so vibrant. Cerulean blue, alizarin crimson, burnt sienna... I used to spend hours on Sunday afternoons just mixing colors. I painted a lot of birds, mostly. Cardinals, blue jays, the occasional goldfinch. We get a lot of goldfinches at the feeder in the backyard. Sarah buys this special thistle seed for them. It's terribly expensive, and it makes an awful mess on the patio, but she loves watching them. Last spring, we had a whole flock of them, bright yellow against the green grass. It looked like a field of dandelions had suddenly taken flight. I tried to capture that in a painting once, but getting the yellow just right is tricky. If you use too much water, it washes out, but if you use too little, it looks muddy. You have to find the perfect balance, like baking bread. My mother baked bread every Tuesday. You could smell the yeast all the way down the street...

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Things that bring me joy. Lately, no. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, and it feels like... like the color has gone out of everything. Even looking at my old watercolor books, the ones I used to study, they don't interest me. They just make me feel heavy. I don't get any pleasure out of them at all right now.

I just sit there at my desk now, and I cannot even decide what simple thing to do next. Sort the returns, check the cart... I just kind of freeze up and don't know where to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't been doing the puzzles or much of anything else to even know if I'd enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nPatient: Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. I haven't had any thoughts like that. Though, speaking of not being alive, we actually just got a massive donation at the library from the estate of Mr. Henderson. He passed away a few weeks ago, bless his heart. He lived in that gorgeous Victorian house on Elm Street, the one with the big weeping willow out in the front yard. I always admired that tree on my walk to work. We had a willow when I was a boy, right by the creek behind our property. My brother Tom and I would spend hours down there catching crawdads. We'd put them in this old, rusty Folgers coffee can. You know, the big metal ones they used to make before everything went to plastic? My mother used to use those old cans to hold her sewing notions. She had one that was just filled to the brim with spare buttons. Every color and shape you could imagine. I used to love running my hands through them when I was little, they made this wonderful clinking sound against the tin... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Oh, right, about... wanting to die. No, no, I don't want to die. I just feel incredibly tired, like I said, and everything feels like a chore. But I definitely haven't thought about anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others lately?\n\nPatient: Worthless? A burden? Well, I suppose I don't do as much around the house as I used to. My wife, Sarah, she's been taking on a lot more of the chores lately. She's a saint, really. We've been married for thirty-five years this coming October. We had our honeymoon in the Poconos, back when those heart-shaped tubs were all the rage. It was autumn, and the leaves were just spectacular. Reds and golds, you know? We took this scenic train ride through the mountains. I remember the conductor had this magnificent handlebar mustache, the kind you only see in old tintype photographs. He told us he used a special wax imported from France to keep it curled just so. I always thought about growing a mustache myself, but Sarah said it would tickle too much. She prefers me clean-shaven. Always has, ever since we met at the roller rink back in seventy-nine. They were playing Donna Summer, and she was wearing these bright yellow leg warmers...\n\nBut, um... where was I going with that? Oh, right. Feeling worthless. No, I wouldn't use those words. I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Joy? Oh, I don't know about joy. I used to love painting, you know. Watercolors. I have this whole set of Windsor and Newton paints my daughter got me for Christmas a few years back. They come in these little half-pans, and the colors are just so vibrant. Cerulean blue, alizarin crimson, burnt sienna... I used to spend hours on Sunday afternoons just mixing colors. I painted a lot of birds, mostly. Cardinals, blue jays, the occasional goldfinch. We get a lot of goldfinches at the feeder in the backyard. Sarah buys this special thistle seed for them. It's terribly expensive, and it makes an awful mess on the patio, but she loves watching them. Last spring, we had a whole flock of them, bright yellow against the green grass. It looked like a field of dandelions had suddenly taken flight. I tried to capture that in a painting once, but getting the yellow just right is tricky. If you use too much water, it washes out, but if you use too little, it looks muddy. You have to find the perfect balance, like baking bread. My mother baked bread every Tuesday. You could smell the yeast all the way down the street...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Things that bring me joy. Lately, no. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, and it feels like... like the color has gone out of everything. Even looking at my old watercolor books, the ones I used to study, they don't interest me. They just make me feel heavy. I don't get any pleasure out of them at all right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Joy? Oh, I don't know about joy. I used to love painting, you know. Watercolors. I have this whole set of Windsor and Newton paints my daughter got me for Christmas a few years back. They come in these little half-pans, and the colors are just so vibrant. Cerulean blue, alizarin crimson, burnt sienna... I used to spend hours on Sunday afternoons just mixing colors. I painted a lot of birds, mostly. Cardinals, blue jays, the occasional goldfinch. We get a lot of goldfinches at the feeder in the backyard. Sarah buys this special thistle seed for them. It's terribly expensive, and it makes an awful mess on the patio, but she loves watching them. Last spring, we had a whole flock of them, bright yellow against the green grass. It looked like a field of dandelions had suddenly taken flight. I tried to capture that in a painting once, but getting the yellow just right is tricky. If you use too much water, it washes out, but if you use too little, it looks muddy. You have to find the perfect balance, like baking bread. My mother baked bread every Tuesday. You could smell the yeast all the way down the street...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Things that bring me joy. Lately, no. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, and it feels like... like the color has gone out of everything. Even looking at my old watercolor books, the ones I used to study, they don't interest me. They just make me feel heavy. I don't get any pleasure out of them at all right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do you find yourself crying or tearful lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Crying? Well, I suppose I do tear up a bit more than usual. The other day, I was watching this documentary on public television about the construction of the Hoover Dam. Fascinating stuff, really. The sheer amount of concrete they used—they said if you paved a two-lane highway with it, it would stretch all the way from San Francisco to New York City. Can you imagine that? And the men who built it, they called them "high scalers." They would dangle on these thin ropes hundreds of feet in the air, drilling into the canyon walls. It made my stomach drop just looking at the old black-and-white footage. I don't know how they had the nerve. My grandfather worked in construction, actually, back in Chicago. Not dams, mostly bricklaying for those big apartment buildings. He had these massive hands, rough as sandpaper, but he could play the piano like you wouldn't believe. Chopin, Beethoven, ragtime... he'd play at family gatherings, and everyone would just stop and listen. He had this old upright piano that was missing a couple of ivory keys, but he knew exactly how to work around them...

I'm sorry, I drifted off again. Um, crying. Yes, well, I don't think I've been crying all that much, really. Maybe just a little misty-eyed here and there, but no more than anyone else, I don't think.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my mind just feels... foggy. Like a thick soup. I work as a library assistant, and lately, I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. Which is really strange for me, because I've always been so particular about my notes. I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? The owner, Mr. Higgins, he always had a jar of peppermints on the counter, the really strong ones. \n\nBut, um... anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I just sit there at my desk now, and I cannot even decide what simple thing to do next. Sort the returns, check the cart... I just kind of freeze up and don't know where to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't been doing the puzzles or much of anything else to even know if I'd enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nPatient: Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nPatient: Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. I haven't had any thoughts like that. Though, speaking of not being alive, we actually just got a massive donation at the library from the estate of Mr. Henderson. He passed away a few weeks ago, bless his heart. He lived in that gorgeous Victorian house on Elm Street, the one with the big weeping willow out in the front yard. I always admired that tree on my walk to work. We had a willow when I was a boy, right by the creek behind our property. My brother Tom and I would spend hours down there catching crawdads. We'd put them in this old, rusty Folgers coffee can. You know, the big metal ones they used to make before everything went to plastic? My mother used to use those old cans to hold her sewing notions. She had one that was just filled to the brim with spare buttons. Every color and shape you could imagine. I used to love running my hands through them when I was little, they made this wonderful clinking sound against the tin... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Oh, right, about... wanting to die. No, no, I don't want to die. I just feel incredibly tired, like I said, and everything feels like a chore. But I definitely haven't thought about anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others lately?\n\nPatient: Worthless? A burden? Well, I suppose I don't do as much around the house as I used to. My wife, Sarah, she's been taking on a lot more of the chores lately. She's a saint, really. We've been married for thirty-five years this coming October. We had our honeymoon in the Poconos, back when those heart-shaped tubs were all the rage. It was autumn, and the leaves were just spectacular. Reds and golds, you know? We took this scenic train ride through the mountains. I remember the conductor had this magnificent handlebar mustache, the kind you only see in old tintype photographs. He told us he used a special wax imported from France to keep it curled just so. I always thought about growing a mustache myself, but Sarah said it would tickle too much. She prefers me clean-shaven. Always has, ever since we met at the roller rink back in seventy-nine. They were playing Donna Summer, and she was wearing these bright yellow leg warmers...\n\nBut, um... where was I going with that? Oh, right. Feeling worthless. No, I wouldn't use those words. I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Joy? Oh, I don't know about joy. I used to love painting, you know. Watercolors. I have this whole set of Windsor and Newton paints my daughter got me for Christmas a few years back. They come in these little half-pans, and the colors are just so vibrant. Cerulean blue, alizarin crimson, burnt sienna... I used to spend hours on Sunday afternoons just mixing colors. I painted a lot of birds, mostly. Cardinals, blue jays, the occasional goldfinch. We get a lot of goldfinches at the feeder in the backyard. Sarah buys this special thistle seed for them. It's terribly expensive, and it makes an awful mess on the patio, but she loves watching them. Last spring, we had a whole flock of them, bright yellow against the green grass. It looked like a field of dandelions had suddenly taken flight. I tried to capture that in a painting once, but getting the yellow just right is tricky. If you use too much water, it washes out, but if you use too little, it looks muddy. You have to find the perfect balance, like baking bread. My mother baked bread every Tuesday. You could smell the yeast all the way down the street...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Things that bring me joy. Lately, no. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, and it feels like... like the color has gone out of everything. Even looking at my old watercolor books, the ones I used to study, they don't interest me. They just make me feel heavy. I don't get any pleasure out of them at all right now.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself crying or tearful lately?\n\nPatient: Crying? Well, I suppose I do tear up a bit more than usual. The other day, I was watching this documentary on public television about the construction of the Hoover Dam. Fascinating stuff, really. The sheer amount of concrete they used—they said if you paved a two-lane highway with it, it would stretch all the way from San Francisco to New York City. Can you imagine that? And the men who built it, they called them \"high scalers.\" They would dangle on these thin ropes hundreds of feet in the air, drilling into the canyon walls. It made my stomach drop just looking at the old black-and-white footage. I don't know how they had the nerve. My grandfather worked in construction, actually, back in Chicago. Not dams, mostly bricklaying for those big apartment buildings. He had these massive hands, rough as sandpaper, but he could play the piano like you wouldn't believe. Chopin, Beethoven, ragtime... he'd play at family gatherings, and everyone would just stop and listen. He had this old upright piano that was missing a couple of ivory keys, but he knew exactly how to work around them...\n\nI'm sorry, I drifted off again. Um, crying. Yes, well, I don't think I've been crying all that much, really. Maybe just a little misty-eyed here and there, but no more than anyone else, I don't think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. How long? Well, let me think. It's been a while now. I suppose it really started around the time I began getting things in order to leave the library. I've been a library assistant for, oh, gosh, almost twenty years. The downtown branch, mostly. We have this beautiful old building, Carnegie style, with these massive oak doors and marble floors that echo when you walk on them. The smell of those old books, it's like vanilla and dust, you know? I was in charge of the periodicals for a long time. People don't read magazines like they used to, but we still got the daily papers in from all over the state. I'd have to put them on those long wooden sticks so people could read them without wrinkling the pages. There was this one gentleman, Mr. Henderson, who came in every morning at nine sharp just to read the Wall Street Journal. Wore a tweed jacket even in July. He always told me he was keeping an eye on pork belly futures, whatever that means. He had this cane with a silver duck head for a handle...

But, um... oh, right, how long I've felt this way. The low mood and that foggy feeling... it's been present most days for about three months now. Right around when I started packing up my desk at the library.

  {
