What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my mind just feels... foggy. Like a thick soup. I work as a library assistant, and lately, I can't even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. Which is really strange for me, because I've always been so particular about my notes. I use these specific index cards, the heavy stock ones you can't really find at the regular office supply stores anymore. You have to order them special from a catalog now. I remember when they used to carry them at the old stationery shop on Main Street before it closed down in '08... or was it '09? The owner, Mr. Higgins, he always had a jar of peppermints on the counter, the really strong ones. \n\nBut, um... anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I just sit there at my desk now, and I cannot even decide what simple thing to do next. Sort the returns, check the cart... I just kind of freeze up and don't know where to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... like all the color has just been drained out of everything. Which is funny to say, because I used to love colors. I have these old watercolor books, you know, the kind with the thick, textured paper that really holds the pigment well. I bought them years ago at a craft fair. There was this lovely woman selling them, she made the paper herself. She had this big loom-looking thing, or a press, I guess it was a press. She explained the whole process to me, how she used recycled cotton rags and pressed them down... it was fascinating. She even gave me a little sample of paper with flower petals pressed into it. I kept it in my favorite book for years, a copy of 'Walden' I've had since high school. \n\nBut, um... oh, the watercolor books. Right. Now when I look at them, they just make me feel heavy. I don't even want to open them. I just sit there and feel... heavy. Instead of interested, you know? It's just all so gray.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I'm not sure I've even tried lately to find out. I used to really like doing the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper. The actual physical paper, mind you, not on a tablet or a phone. My nephew bought me one of those iPads a couple of years ago to do puzzles on, but it's just not the same without a pen. I always use a blue Pilot G2 pen, the zero-point-seven millimeter one. Black ink just looks too harsh on the gray newsprint, and pencil smudges absolutely everywhere, especially if you're left-handed. My older brother is left-handed, actually. I remember he used to get silver graphite smeared all over the side of his palm when we were in grade school. The teachers used to get so frustrated with him because his spelling tests were always a big blurry mess... \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't been doing the puzzles or much of anything else to even know if I'd enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, isn't it? Losing interest. Like with my garden. I used to care a great deal about my tomatoes. I had these beautiful heirloom varieties—Brandywines and Cherokee Purples. The Cherokee Purples get so big and heavy you have to tie them up with special twine or they'll just snap the stakes right in half. I used to buy my twine from the hardware store two towns over because the local place only carried that cheap nylon string that cuts right into the plant stems. The fellow who ran the hardware store, Arthur, he knew everything about gardening. He told me once that the secret to good tomatoes was crushed eggshells in the soil. Said his grandmother taught him that during the Depression when they couldn't afford proper fertilizer... \n\nBut, um... anyway. Have I lost interest? I don't know. I just don't have the energy to care about the tomatoes or the crossword or much of anything right now. It just all seems like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has certainly noticed something. She says I'm leaving my meals half-finished, which is very unlike me. I used to never waste a meal, especially not rice and soup. We have this specific brand of jasmine rice we always buy, the one with the blue elephant on the bag. It comes in these massive twenty-pound sacks from the Asian market downtown. The market is owned by this wonderful family, the Nguyens. Mr. Nguyen always remembers exactly what aisle everything is in, even though the store is packed to the rafters. He told me once they get their shipments straight from Thailand every Tuesday morning. I used to love going there early just to see all the fresh produce being unloaded, the boxes of dragon fruit and those spiky rambutan things... \n\nBut, um... right, my weight. I suppose I must be losing some if I'm not eating. I just... I have to actually remind myself to eat these days. The food is right there, but I just don't have the appetite for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or nervous most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. My sister, now she's the anxious one. She's always worrying about something or other. Just last week she called me in an absolute state because she couldn't find her reading glasses. She has these tortoiseshell frames she bought when she was visiting our cousin in Seattle. Cousin Martha, that is. Martha moved out there in the late eighties to work for Boeing. She was always very good at math, Martha was. Won the state mathletics competition in high school. They gave her this shiny brass trophy with a little owl on top. It sat on her mother's mantelpiece for years, right next to this old pendulum clock that chimed on the quarter hour. It was a beautiful clock, but the ticking drove me absolutely up the wall whenever we stayed over...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Nervous. No, I don't really feel nervous or anything. Just... heavy, I guess. Like everything is just too much trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, I see a lot of guilt at work, actually. I work at the library, mostly behind the circulation desk, and you wouldn't believe the looks on people's faces when they bring back an overdue book. They look like they've robbed a bank! Just last week, Mrs. Gable came in. She reads those cozy mysteries, the ones that always have a cat on the cover. Usually they're set in some small coastal town in Maine where the local baker or the bookstore owner is secretly solving murders. I always wonder how those fictional towns have any population left with all the murders going on, honestly. Anyway, she had spilled tea on one of the pages. Earl Grey, she said. I prefer a nice chamomile myself. My grandmother used to grow her own chamomile in these wooden window boxes she painted a lovely shade of robin's egg blue. She'd dry the flowers on newspaper out in the sunroom, and the whole house would smell like apples... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, do I feel guilty about things. No, not really. I haven't done anything to feel guilty about. I just don't have the energy to feel much of anything at all right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to be alive lately?\n\nPatient: Not wanting to be alive? Oh, heavens no. I haven't had any thoughts like that. Though, speaking of not being alive, we actually just got a massive donation at the library from the estate of Mr. Henderson. He passed away a few weeks ago, bless his heart. He lived in that gorgeous Victorian house on Elm Street, the one with the big weeping willow out in the front yard. I always admired that tree on my walk to work. We had a willow when I was a boy, right by the creek behind our property. My brother Tom and I would spend hours down there catching crawdads. We'd put them in this old, rusty Folgers coffee can. You know, the big metal ones they used to make before everything went to plastic? My mother used to use those old cans to hold her sewing notions. She had one that was just filled to the brim with spare buttons. Every color and shape you could imagine. I used to love running my hands through them when I was little, they made this wonderful clinking sound against the tin... \n\nBut, um... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Oh, right, about... wanting to die. No, no, I don't want to die. I just feel incredibly tired, like I said, and everything feels like a chore. But I definitely haven't thought about anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others lately?\n\nPatient: Worthless? A burden? Well, I suppose I don't do as much around the house as I used to. My wife, Sarah, she's been taking on a lot more of the chores lately. She's a saint, really. We've been married for thirty-five years this coming October. We had our honeymoon in the Poconos, back when those heart-shaped tubs were all the rage. It was autumn, and the leaves were just spectacular. Reds and golds, you know? We took this scenic train ride through the mountains. I remember the conductor had this magnificent handlebar mustache, the kind you only see in old tintype photographs. He told us he used a special wax imported from France to keep it curled just so. I always thought about growing a mustache myself, but Sarah said it would tickle too much. She prefers me clean-shaven. Always has, ever since we met at the roller rink back in seventy-nine. They were playing Donna Summer, and she was wearing these bright yellow leg warmers...\n\nBut, um... where was I going with that? Oh, right. Feeling worthless. No, I wouldn't use those words. I don't feel like a burden, exactly. Just... useless, maybe? Like I'm just taking up space because I can't muster the energy to do the things I normally do. But I don't think I'm worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Joy? Oh, I don't know about joy. I used to love painting, you know. Watercolors. I have this whole set of Windsor and Newton paints my daughter got me for Christmas a few years back. They come in these little half-pans, and the colors are just so vibrant. Cerulean blue, alizarin crimson, burnt sienna... I used to spend hours on Sunday afternoons just mixing colors. I painted a lot of birds, mostly. Cardinals, blue jays, the occasional goldfinch. We get a lot of goldfinches at the feeder in the backyard. Sarah buys this special thistle seed for them. It's terribly expensive, and it makes an awful mess on the patio, but she loves watching them. Last spring, we had a whole flock of them, bright yellow against the green grass. It looked like a field of dandelions had suddenly taken flight. I tried to capture that in a painting once, but getting the yellow just right is tricky. If you use too much water, it washes out, but if you use too little, it looks muddy. You have to find the perfect balance, like baking bread. My mother baked bread every Tuesday. You could smell the yeast all the way down the street...\n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Things that bring me joy. Lately, no. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, and it feels like... like the color has gone out of everything. Even looking at my old watercolor books, the ones I used to study, they don't interest me. They just make me feel heavy. I don't get any pleasure out of them at all right now.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself crying or tearful lately?\n\nPatient: Crying? Well, I suppose I do tear up a bit more than usual. The other day, I was watching this documentary on public television about the construction of the Hoover Dam. Fascinating stuff, really. The sheer amount of concrete they used—they said if you paved a two-lane highway with it, it would stretch all the way from San Francisco to New York City. Can you imagine that? And the men who built it, they called them \"high scalers.\" They would dangle on these thin ropes hundreds of feet in the air, drilling into the canyon walls. It made my stomach drop just looking at the old black-and-white footage. I don't know how they had the nerve. My grandfather worked in construction, actually, back in Chicago. Not dams, mostly bricklaying for those big apartment buildings. He had these massive hands, rough as sandpaper, but he could play the piano like you wouldn't believe. Chopin, Beethoven, ragtime... he'd play at family gatherings, and everyone would just stop and listen. He had this old upright piano that was missing a couple of ivory keys, but he knew exactly how to work around them...\n\nI'm sorry, I drifted off again. Um, crying. Yes, well, I don't think I've been crying all that much, really. Maybe just a little misty-eyed here and there, but no more than anyone else, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think. It's been a while now. I suppose it really started around the time I began getting things in order to leave the library. I've been a library assistant for, oh, gosh, almost twenty years. The downtown branch, mostly. We have this beautiful old building, Carnegie style, with these massive oak doors and marble floors that echo when you walk on them. The smell of those old books, it's like vanilla and dust, you know? I was in charge of the periodicals for a long time. People don't read magazines like they used to, but we still got the daily papers in from all over the state. I'd have to put them on those long wooden sticks so people could read them without wrinkling the pages. There was this one gentleman, Mr. Henderson, who came in every morning at nine sharp just to read the Wall Street Journal. Wore a tweed jacket even in July. He always told me he was keeping an eye on pork belly futures, whatever that means. He had this cane with a silver duck head for a handle...\n\nBut, um... oh, right, how long I've felt this way. The low mood and that foggy feeling... it's been present most days for about three months now. Right around when I started packing up my desk at the library.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Yes, I suppose I do. My body just feels so slow lately. Even walking down to the corner store for a gallon of milk feels like... well, it takes more effort than it should. The corner store is run by this lovely couple, the Patels. They've been there since my daughter was in grade school. They used to sell these little penny candies, Swedish Fish and those wax bottles with the colored syrup inside. Sarah loved those. She'd bite the tops off and drink the syrup, and then chew on the wax until I made her spit it out. Kids will chew on anything, won't they? It reminds me of my dog, Buster. He was a golden retriever mix, sweetest thing, but he had a terrible habit of chewing on my good leather shoes. I had this one pair of wingtips I bought for a cousin's wedding... beautiful cordovan leather. I left them by the front door for five minutes, and Buster just went to town on the heel. I had to take them to the cobbler on 4th Street, Mr. Rossi. He was a miracle worker, that man. His shop smelled strongly of shoe polish and glue, and he had these old vintage posters of Italy on the walls. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sicily. He always had an espresso machine humming in the back...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, moving slowly. Yes. Even at work, before I left, just sorting the papers on my desk... it felt like I was moving through molasses. Everything just takes so much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking slowly? Well, now that you mention it, maybe I do. It kind of goes along with the rest of it, you know? Like I was saying, my body just feels slow, like even walking to the corner store or sorting papers on my desk takes more effort than it should. It's like my brain is working at half speed, too. It reminds me of this old manual typewriter we had at the library when I first started. An Underwood, I think it was. Heavy black metal, beautiful machine, but the keys were so stiff. You really had to press down hard to make the letters strike the ribbon. We used it for typing up index cards for the card catalog. People don't know what a card catalog is anymore, do they? Everything is on the computer now. But back then, we had these long wooden cabinets with hundreds of little drawers, and every single book had a card. Three cards, actually: author, title, and subject. It was a whole system. Melvil Dewey, you know, the Dewey Decimal System. He was a complicated figure, Dewey. Did a lot for libraries, but he had some... well, some controversial views. But anyway, typing those cards, you couldn't make a mistake, or you'd have to start all over again. No backspace key on an Underwood. My fingers used to ache by the end of the day.\n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Drifting off. Yes, speaking slowly. I suppose my words just don't come out as fast as they used to. Everything just feels... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Thoughts of harming myself? Oh, no. No, I haven't had anything like that. I don't think I could ever do that. It reminds me of this book we had in the special collections at the library, a first edition of Sylvia Plath's *The Bell Jar*. It was kept behind the glass, you know, because of how valuable it was. We had a whole section of rare books that you had to wear white cotton gloves just to handle. It always made me feel like a jewel thief or something, slipping on those little gloves. There was this one time a researcher came all the way from London just to look at our collection of 19th-century botanical illustrations. Beautiful hand-painted plates of ferns and orchids. He spent three days just sitting at the oak table by the window, turning the pages so carefully. He had this accent, very proper, and he drank tea out of a thermos he brought with him. Earl Grey, I think it was. You could smell the bergamot when he unscrewed the lid. I always meant to ask him where he got that thermos, it was this lovely hunter green color with a silver cup... \n\nBut, um, no. To answer your question, no thoughts of wanting to die or anything like that. Just... just the heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that things you used to enjoy doing are no longer giving you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? Well, yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... I don't know, like the color has just gone out of everything. I used to love my old watercolor books. I'd sit there for hours looking at the different techniques and the landscapes. I bought most of those books at this little art supply shop downtown, Henderson's. Mr. Henderson ran it himself, always wore a little plaid bow tie, every single day. He had this incredible parrot in the shop, an African Grey named Picasso. Smartest bird you ever saw. If you walked in and the bell on the door rang, Picasso would say, \"Welcome, Rembrandt!\" in this raspy little voice. And he could mimic the sound of the old mechanical cash register perfectly. It used to confuse the customers terribly, they'd reach for their wallets thinking they were being rung up when Mr. Henderson was all the way in the back aisle stocking canvases. Parrots live a long time, you know. Sometimes fifty, sixty years. I read once that Winston Churchill had a parrot that outlived him by decades, and it would still curse at people in Churchill's exact voice. Isn't that something? Just sitting on a perch, repeating history... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. My mind just wanders. You were asking about enjoying things. Yes, the watercolors. Now, even when I open those old watercolor books, I don't feel interested at all. I just look at the pages and I just feel... heavy. There's no spark to it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood and lack of interest in activities are present most of the day or nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yes, I'd say so. It's been most days, really, for... oh, about three months now. It started right around the time I began preparing to leave the library. The low mood, that fogginess I was telling you about. Leaving the library... it was quite a process. You accumulate so much over thirty years. Not just the books, but the little things. I had this mug on my desk, it had a picture of a cat reading a newspaper, and it said 'I'm booked for the weekend.' A silly little thing, but it was a gift from one of our regular patrons, Mrs. Gable. She used to come in every Tuesday at 10 AM sharp, rain or shine. She always wore these brightly colored raincoats, even in the middle of summer. Yellow, cherry red, sometimes a vibrant teal. She said it was because she wanted to make sure the cars could see her when she crossed the street, but I think she just liked the colors. She read exclusively cozy mysteries. You know the kind, where the baker or the knitting shop owner solves the murder? She must have read every single Agatha Christie we had, twice over. I remember when we had to replace our copy of *The Murder of Roger Ackroyd* because the spine completely split down the middle. Book binding is a fascinating art, really. The way they used to sew the signatures together by hand... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. Yes, the low mood. It's been most days for the last three months, since I started getting ready to leave. Just... present all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is mildly bothersome, moderately debilitating, or severely impairing your daily life?\n\nPatient: Moderately debilitating, I suppose. It's... it's getting in the way of things I really need to do. I have all these lecture plans for the local historical society, travel notes I wanted to organize, and even just simple household paperwork, the bills and the mail. It's all just piling up on the dining room table because I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to. I look at the piles and I just don't know where to start. It reminds me of the time we had a pipe burst in the basement of the library, right over the local history archives. Oh, it was a disaster. The water was ankle-deep, and we had to form a human chain to pass the boxes of documents up the stairs. We had to lay out all these 19th-century land deeds and census records on every available flat surface to dry. We had fans running for weeks. The smell of damp paper is something you never quite forget, it's sort of sweet and musty all at once. We had to hire a specialized restoration company from Chicago to come in and freeze-dry the most badly damaged ledgers. They use this vacuum chamber process, it's really quite ingenious how they pull the moisture out without destroying the ink. The lead restorer, a woman named Eleanor, told me she once worked on a collection of letters that had been salvaged from a shipwreck off the coast of Maine. Imagine that, pulling history right out of the ocean... \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yes, the mail and the notes are just sitting there. I just can't seem to get my thoughts in order to tackle it. It's very frustrating, not being able to manage my own desk when I used to manage an entire archives room.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is interfering with your daily life in a way that makes it hard to function at all?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, it really is interfering. I wouldn't say I can't function at all, but... well, the lecture plans and those travel notes I mentioned, and even just the simple household bills. I've let them all just pile up. I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to, so I just avoid it completely. \n\nThe travel notes were actually for a train trip I've been meaning to take up the East Coast. Have you ever taken the sleeper cars on the train? They are really quite an experience. My uncle Arthur used to work as a conductor on the old Pullman sleeper cars back in the late fifties. He had this immaculate navy blue uniform with these heavy brass buttons that he polished every single Sunday afternoon while listening to the baseball game on the radio. He'd tell these wonderful stories about the people he'd meet on the overnight routes. Politicians, businessmen, movie stars. He swore up and down that he once spilled a cup of black coffee right on Clark Gable's leather shoes. Or maybe it was Cary Grant? One of those handsome leading men. Anyway, he said the man was incredibly gracious about it, just dabbed it with a cloth napkin and left him a five-dollar tip anyway. You don't see that kind of grace much anymore. And they certainly don't make trains like they used to, with the real dining cars and the white linen tablecloths and the silver service. Now it's all just microwaved hot dogs wrapped in plastic in the cafe car. It's a terrible shame how we've lost that sense of elegance in travel, don't you think? People just rush everywhere now, nobody wants to actually enjoy the journey... \n\nOh, dear. I'm rambling again. I apologize, my brain just snags on a thought and runs away with it. You were asking if it's hard to function. Yes. It is. I just look at that pile of paperwork and notes on the dining table and I just... I can't organize any of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low mood and lack of interest in activities are severely impairing your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, severely... I don't know if I'd use the word severely, but as I was saying, it's certainly a moderate impairment. I just... I have let the lecture plans, those travel notes I was telling you about, and even just the simple household paperwork, the bills, I've just let them all pile up. I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to. \n\nIt's strange, losing that knack for organization. My whole life was organized alphabetically and by the Dewey Decimal System. Did you know Melvil Dewey was actually quite a controversial figure? Brilliant system, of course, but the man himself... well, he was forced out of the American Library Association. He had this club in upstate New York, Lake Placid I think, and they had these terrible exclusionary policies. Wouldn't let Jewish people or minorities stay there. And he was notoriously inappropriate with female librarians. It's one of those difficult historical things, isn't it? When someone creates something so universally useful, but their personal character is deeply flawed. We had a whole seminar on it once at a state library conference in Albany. Albany is a beautiful city in the fall, by the way. The leaves turning along the Hudson River... we took a riverboat tour one evening, and they served this wonderful spiced apple cider...\n\nBut yes, the impairment. It's just that I can't organize myself anymore. The paperwork just sits there on the table.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, well, I don't think I've been crying all that much, really. Maybe just a little misty-eyed here and there, but no more than anyone else, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think. It's been a while now. I suppose it really started around the time I began getting things in order to leave the library. I've been a library assistant for, oh, gosh, almost twenty years. The downtown branch, mostly. We have this beautiful old building, Carnegie style, with these massive oak doors and marble floors that echo when you walk on them. The smell of those old books, it's like vanilla and dust, you know? I was in charge of the periodicals for a long time. People don't read magazines like they used to, but we still got the daily papers in from all over the state. I'd have to put them on those long wooden sticks so people could read them without wrinkling the pages. There was this one gentleman, Mr. Henderson, who came in every morning at nine sharp just to read the Wall Street Journal. Wore a tweed jacket even in July. He always told me he was keeping an eye on pork belly futures, whatever that means. He had this cane with a silver duck head for a handle...\n\nBut, um... oh, right, how long I've felt this way. The low mood and that foggy feeling... it's been present most days for about three months now. Right around when I started packing up my desk at the library.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Yes, I suppose I do. My body just feels so slow lately. Even walking down to the corner store for a gallon of milk feels like... well, it takes more effort than it should. The corner store is run by this lovely couple, the Patels. They've been there since my daughter was in grade school. They used to sell these little penny candies, Swedish Fish and those wax bottles with the colored syrup inside. Sarah loved those. She'd bite the tops off and drink the syrup, and then chew on the wax until I made her spit it out. Kids will chew on anything, won't they? It reminds me of my dog, Buster. He was a golden retriever mix, sweetest thing, but he had a terrible habit of chewing on my good leather shoes. I had this one pair of wingtips I bought for a cousin's wedding... beautiful cordovan leather. I left them by the front door for five minutes, and Buster just went to town on the heel. I had to take them to the cobbler on 4th Street, Mr. Rossi. He was a miracle worker, that man. His shop smelled strongly of shoe polish and glue, and he had these old vintage posters of Italy on the walls. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sicily. He always had an espresso machine humming in the back...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, moving slowly. Yes. Even at work, before I left, just sorting the papers on my desk... it felt like I was moving through molasses. Everything just takes so much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking slowly? Well, now that you mention it, maybe I do. It kind of goes along with the rest of it, you know? Like I was saying, my body just feels slow, like even walking to the corner store or sorting papers on my desk takes more effort than it should. It's like my brain is working at half speed, too. It reminds me of this old manual typewriter we had at the library when I first started. An Underwood, I think it was. Heavy black metal, beautiful machine, but the keys were so stiff. You really had to press down hard to make the letters strike the ribbon. We used it for typing up index cards for the card catalog. People don't know what a card catalog is anymore, do they? Everything is on the computer now. But back then, we had these long wooden cabinets with hundreds of little drawers, and every single book had a card. Three cards, actually: author, title, and subject. It was a whole system. Melvil Dewey, you know, the Dewey Decimal System. He was a complicated figure, Dewey. Did a lot for libraries, but he had some... well, some controversial views. But anyway, typing those cards, you couldn't make a mistake, or you'd have to start all over again. No backspace key on an Underwood. My fingers used to ache by the end of the day.\n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Drifting off. Yes, speaking slowly. I suppose my words just don't come out as fast as they used to. Everything just feels... heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Thoughts of harming myself? Oh, no. No, I haven't had anything like that. I don't think I could ever do that. It reminds me of this book we had in the special collections at the library, a first edition of Sylvia Plath's *The Bell Jar*. It was kept behind the glass, you know, because of how valuable it was. We had a whole section of rare books that you had to wear white cotton gloves just to handle. It always made me feel like a jewel thief or something, slipping on those little gloves. There was this one time a researcher came all the way from London just to look at our collection of 19th-century botanical illustrations. Beautiful hand-painted plates of ferns and orchids. He spent three days just sitting at the oak table by the window, turning the pages so carefully. He had this accent, very proper, and he drank tea out of a thermos he brought with him. Earl Grey, I think it was. You could smell the bergamot when he unscrewed the lid. I always meant to ask him where he got that thermos, it was this lovely hunter green color with a silver cup... \n\nBut, um, no. To answer your question, no thoughts of wanting to die or anything like that. Just... just the heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that things you used to enjoy doing are no longer giving you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? Well, yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Most days lately, I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like... I don't know, like the color has just gone out of everything. I used to love my old watercolor books. I'd sit there for hours looking at the different techniques and the landscapes. I bought most of those books at this little art supply shop downtown, Henderson's. Mr. Henderson ran it himself, always wore a little plaid bow tie, every single day. He had this incredible parrot in the shop, an African Grey named Picasso. Smartest bird you ever saw. If you walked in and the bell on the door rang, Picasso would say, \"Welcome, Rembrandt!\" in this raspy little voice. And he could mimic the sound of the old mechanical cash register perfectly. It used to confuse the customers terribly, they'd reach for their wallets thinking they were being rung up when Mr. Henderson was all the way in the back aisle stocking canvases. Parrots live a long time, you know. Sometimes fifty, sixty years. I read once that Winston Churchill had a parrot that outlived him by decades, and it would still curse at people in Churchill's exact voice. Isn't that something? Just sitting on a perch, repeating history... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. My mind just wanders. You were asking about enjoying things. Yes, the watercolors. Now, even when I open those old watercolor books, I don't feel interested at all. I just look at the pages and I just feel... heavy. There's no spark to it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood and lack of interest in activities are present most of the day or nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yes, I'd say so. It's been most days, really, for... oh, about three months now. It started right around the time I began preparing to leave the library. The low mood, that fogginess I was telling you about. Leaving the library... it was quite a process. You accumulate so much over thirty years. Not just the books, but the little things. I had this mug on my desk, it had a picture of a cat reading a newspaper, and it said 'I'm booked for the weekend.' A silly little thing, but it was a gift from one of our regular patrons, Mrs. Gable. She used to come in every Tuesday at 10 AM sharp, rain or shine. She always wore these brightly colored raincoats, even in the middle of summer. Yellow, cherry red, sometimes a vibrant teal. She said it was because she wanted to make sure the cars could see her when she crossed the street, but I think she just liked the colors. She read exclusively cozy mysteries. You know the kind, where the baker or the knitting shop owner solves the murder? She must have read every single Agatha Christie we had, twice over. I remember when we had to replace our copy of *The Murder of Roger Ackroyd* because the spine completely split down the middle. Book binding is a fascinating art, really. The way they used to sew the signatures together by hand... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. Yes, the low mood. It's been most days for the last three months, since I started getting ready to leave. Just... present all the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is mildly bothersome, moderately debilitating, or severely impairing your daily life?\n\nPatient: Moderately debilitating, I suppose. It's... it's getting in the way of things I really need to do. I have all these lecture plans for the local historical society, travel notes I wanted to organize, and even just simple household paperwork, the bills and the mail. It's all just piling up on the dining room table because I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to. I look at the piles and I just don't know where to start. It reminds me of the time we had a pipe burst in the basement of the library, right over the local history archives. Oh, it was a disaster. The water was ankle-deep, and we had to form a human chain to pass the boxes of documents up the stairs. We had to lay out all these 19th-century land deeds and census records on every available flat surface to dry. We had fans running for weeks. The smell of damp paper is something you never quite forget, it's sort of sweet and musty all at once. We had to hire a specialized restoration company from Chicago to come in and freeze-dry the most badly damaged ledgers. They use this vacuum chamber process, it's really quite ingenious how they pull the moisture out without destroying the ink. The lead restorer, a woman named Eleanor, told me she once worked on a collection of letters that had been salvaged from a shipwreck off the coast of Maine. Imagine that, pulling history right out of the ocean... \n\nBut, um, where was I? Oh, the paperwork. Yes, the mail and the notes are just sitting there. I just can't seem to get my thoughts in order to tackle it. It's very frustrating, not being able to manage my own desk when I used to manage an entire archives room.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is interfering with your daily life in a way that makes it hard to function at all?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, it really is interfering. I wouldn't say I can't function at all, but... well, the lecture plans and those travel notes I mentioned, and even just the simple household bills. I've let them all just pile up. I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to, so I just avoid it completely. \n\nThe travel notes were actually for a train trip I've been meaning to take up the East Coast. Have you ever taken the sleeper cars on the train? They are really quite an experience. My uncle Arthur used to work as a conductor on the old Pullman sleeper cars back in the late fifties. He had this immaculate navy blue uniform with these heavy brass buttons that he polished every single Sunday afternoon while listening to the baseball game on the radio. He'd tell these wonderful stories about the people he'd meet on the overnight routes. Politicians, businessmen, movie stars. He swore up and down that he once spilled a cup of black coffee right on Clark Gable's leather shoes. Or maybe it was Cary Grant? One of those handsome leading men. Anyway, he said the man was incredibly gracious about it, just dabbed it with a cloth napkin and left him a five-dollar tip anyway. You don't see that kind of grace much anymore. And they certainly don't make trains like they used to, with the real dining cars and the white linen tablecloths and the silver service. Now it's all just microwaved hot dogs wrapped in plastic in the cafe car. It's a terrible shame how we've lost that sense of elegance in travel, don't you think? People just rush everywhere now, nobody wants to actually enjoy the journey... \n\nOh, dear. I'm rambling again. I apologize, my brain just snags on a thought and runs away with it. You were asking if it's hard to function. Yes. It is. I just look at that pile of paperwork and notes on the dining table and I just... I can't organize any of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low mood and lack of interest in activities are severely impairing your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, severely... I don't know if I'd use the word severely, but as I was saying, it's certainly a moderate impairment. I just... I have let the lecture plans, those travel notes I was telling you about, and even just the simple household paperwork, the bills, I've just let them all pile up. I cannot reliably organize myself the way I used to. \n\nIt's strange, losing that knack for organization. My whole life was organized alphabetically and by the Dewey Decimal System. Did you know Melvil Dewey was actually quite a controversial figure? Brilliant system, of course, but the man himself... well, he was forced out of the American Library Association. He had this club in upstate New York, Lake Placid I think, and they had these terrible exclusionary policies. Wouldn't let Jewish people or minorities stay there. And he was notoriously inappropriate with female librarians. It's one of those difficult historical things, isn't it? When someone creates something so universally useful, but their personal character is deeply flawed. We had a whole seminar on it once at a state library conference in Albany. Albany is a beautiful city in the fall, by the way. The leaves turning along the Hudson River... we took a riverboat tour one evening, and they served this wonderful spiced apple cider...\n\nBut yes, the impairment. It's just that I can't organize myself anymore. The paperwork just sits there on the table."
  }
